\mu^{*}\colon\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}(Y\times X)\rightarrow\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}(X_{F_{1}})
is surjective. Since
\varphi_{F_{1}}
is isotropic, the target contains
l_{0}
. By the definition of
\mu
, it follows that there exists an indecomposable element of
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}}(Y\times X)
involving
h^{0}\times l_{0}
. Applying
D^{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1,0}
to this element, we get an indecomposable element
\beta\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}-{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}+1}(Y\times X)
that involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\varphi)}-1}\times l_{0}
. Suppose now that
\alpha
is an element of
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}(X\times Y)
that involves
h^{0}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}
. We can then write
\alpha=(h^{0}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1})+\lambda(l_{a}\times h^{0})+\alpha^{\prime}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
and some
\alpha^{\prime}\in(R_{X\times Y})_{d_{X}+{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}
that involves neither
h^{0}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}
or
l_{a}\times h^{0}
. Replacing
\alpha
with
\mathrm{ess}(\alpha)
if needed, we can further assume that
\alpha^{\prime}
involves no standard basis elements with
h^{0}
as the second factor. Consider now the composition
\alpha\circ\beta\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2})
. Since
\beta
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}\times l_{0}
, and since
\alpha
involves
h^{0}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}
, Lemma
5.1
shows that
\alpha\circ\beta
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}
. Applying Proposition
7.13
to an indecomposable element
\eta\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2})
involving
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{1}(\psi)}-1}
and satisfying
\eta\subset\alpha\circ\beta
, we then get that
\alpha\circ\beta
also involves
l_{0}\times h^{0}
. Since
\alpha^{\prime}
involves no standard basis elements with
h^{0}
as the second factor, Lemma
5.1
then tells us that
\lambda(l_{a}\times h^{0})\circ\beta\neq 0
, and so
a\geq 0
and
\lambda=1
, proving the lemma. ∎

Our version of Vishik’s result is now the following:

Theorem 8.3
.

Suppose that there are integers
0\leq m<r
and
0\leq n<r^{\prime}
such that for every separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>m
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>n
. If
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
admits an element
\Lambda
with
a(\Lambda)=m
, then
\Lambda[n-m]
is an element of
\mathrm{MDT}(\psi)
.

Proof.

Let
s
and
t
be the unique integers with
{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}\leq m<{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}
and
{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}\leq n<{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}
. By Lemma
2.3
and Proposition
7.14
, our hypotheses are then equivalent to:

•

For every separable extension
K/F
,
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}
;

•

\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
admits an element
\Lambda^{\prime}
with
a(\Lambda^{\prime})={\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
.

We may thus assume that
m={\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
and
n={\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}
. Let us set

a:=d_{X}-d_{Y}-{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}+{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}-1

and

b:={\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}-1-{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}=a+d_{Y}-d_{X}-{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}.

We make the following claim:

Claim.
In the above situation,
a\geq{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
, and there exists a cycle
\beta\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b}(X\times Y)
with the following properties:

(i)

\beta
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
and
l_{a}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}
;

(ii)

If
\beta
involves
h^{u}\times l_{v}
(resp.
l_{u}\times h^{v}
) for some integers
u,v
, then
u_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda
(resp.
(d_{X}-u)^{\mathrm{up}}\in\Lambda
);

(iii)

\beta
involves no terms of the form
h^{u}\times h^{v}
or
l_{u}\times l_{v}
for integers
u
and
v
.

Before proving it, let us first show how the claim yields the desired result. Let
\beta
be as in the claim, and let
\beta^{t}
be its image under pushforward along the canonical isomorphism
X\times Y\rightarrow Y\times X
. Set

\gamma:=(h^{{\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\varphi)}-1}\times h^{0})\beta^{t}\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b-({\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\psi)}-1)}(Y\times X).

By (i),
\beta
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
and
\gamma
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}\times l_{a}
. Using (ii) and (iii), together with Lemma
5.1
, one then checks that
\gamma\circ\beta\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}-{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+a}(X^{2})
involves

(h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}\times l_{a})\circ(h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1})=h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{a}.

By Lemma
7.4
it follows that
a<{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}
. But since
\beta
involves
l_{a}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}
, (ii) tells us that
(d_{X}-a)^{\mathrm{up}}
. Since
a(\Lambda)={\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
, Corollary
7.15
then gives that
a\geq{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}-1
, and so
a={\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}-1
. Let us now consider the cycle

\eta:=(h^{{\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)}-1}\times h^{0})\beta\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b-({\mathfrak{i}_{s}(\varphi)}-1)}(X\times Y).

By (i) and the preceding remarks
\beta^{t}
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}\times l_{a}=h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}-1}
and
\eta
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}-1}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
. Using properties (ii) and (iii) of
\beta
, together with Lemma
5.1
, one then checks that
\eta\circ\beta^{t}\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}(Y^{2})
involves

(h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}-1}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1})\circ(h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}-1})=h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}.

Let
\alpha
be the unique indecomposable element of
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2})
which is essential and involves
l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
. By the preceding remarks, both
\alpha^{t}
and
D^{{\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\psi)}-1,0}(\eta\circ\beta^{t})\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2})
involve
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
, and so
\alpha^{t}\subset D^{{\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\psi)}-1,0}(\eta\circ\beta^{t})
. We now claim that
\Lambda(\alpha)=\Lambda[{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}-{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}]=\Lambda[m-n]
. Since
\alpha
is indecomposable, this will prove what we want. Let
0\leq i<r^{\prime}
. If
\alpha
involves
h^{i}\times l_{i}
, then
\alpha^{t}
involves
l_{i}\times h^{i}
, and so
\eta\circ\beta^{t}
involves
l_{i+({\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1)}\times h^{i}
. By Lemma
5.1
,
\beta^{t}\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b}(Y\times X)
must then involve
l_{i+({\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1)}\times h^{i+({\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1)-b}
, and so
\beta
involves
h^{i+({\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1)-b}\times l_{i+({\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1)}
. Setting
k:={\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}-{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\varphi)}=b-({\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\psi)}-1)
, we see that
\alpha
involves
h^{i}\times l_{i}
only if
\beta
involves
h^{i-k}\times l_{i+({\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1)}
. By property (ii) of
\beta
, this implies that
\mathrm{i}_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda(\alpha)
only if
(i-k)_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda
. In other words,
\Lambda(\alpha)_{\mathrm{lo}}\subseteq\Lambda[k]_{\mathrm{lo}}
. A similar calculation shows that
\Lambda(\alpha)^{\mathrm{up}}\subseteq\Lambda[k]^{\mathrm{up}}
, and so
\Lambda(\alpha)\subseteq\Lambda[k]
. Now since
\alpha
involves
l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
, we have
(d_{Y}-({\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1))^{\mathrm{up}}\in\Lambda(\alpha)
. By Proposition
7.13
, it follows that
{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda(\alpha)
. Since
a(\Lambda(k))=a(\Lambda)+k={\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+k={\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}
, we then have that
a(\Lambda(\alpha))={\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}
. As
\Lambda(\alpha)
is an elememt of
\mathrm{MDT}(\psi)
, this puts us in a position to switch the roles of
\varphi
and
\psi
in the preceding arguments. In doing so, the roles of
\Lambda
and
\Lambda(\alpha)
are switched, and the role played by
k
is assumed by
-k
. The preceding arguments then tells us that
\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda(\alpha)[-k]
. In other words,
\Lambda[k]\subseteq\Lambda(\alpha)
, and so
\Lambda(\alpha)=\Lambda[k]=\Lambda[{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}-{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}]
, as desired.

It remains to prove the claim. We first show by induction on
s
that there exists an element
\nu\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b}(X\times Y)
involving
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
. The case where
s=1
was already done as part of the proof of Lemma
8.2
. Suppose now that
s\geq 2
, and let
F_{1}=F(\varphi)
. Set
\varphi^{\prime}:=\varphi_{1}=(\varphi_{F_{1}})_{\mathrm{an}}
and
\psi^{\prime}:=(\psi_{F_{1}})_{\mathrm{an}}
. By Lemma
8.1
,
{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{1}})}
lies in the nondefective splitting pattern of
\psi
. Moreover, for any separable extension
K/F_{1}
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi^{\prime}_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi^{\prime}_{K})}\geq{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{1}})}-1
. Set
X^{\prime}:=X_{\varphi^{\prime}}
and
Y^{\prime}:=X_{\psi^{\prime}}
. If necessary, we choose orientations of
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
, and use these to orient
X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{1}})}}(X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime})
has an element involving
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{1}})}}
. Using the map
g
from Proposition
6.2
, we then get a cycle in
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b}((X\times Y)_{F_{1}})
involving
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
. As in the proof of Lemma
8.2
,
[
3
, Cor. 57.11]
allows us to lift this to a cycle
\xi\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{2d_{X}+b}(X^{2}\times Y)
involving
h^{0}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
. In
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+d_{Y}}(X^{2}\times Y)
, we also have the element
\xi^{\prime}:=\alpha_{\Lambda}\times h^{0}
. Using that
\Lambda(\alpha)={\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
, one readily checks that the product
\xi\xi^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b}(X^{2}\times Y)
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{0}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}
. Pushing forward along the projection from
X^{2}\times Y
to the product of its two outer factors then gives the desired cycle
\nu
. Let us now set
\beta:=\mathrm{ess}(\nu\circ\alpha_{\Lambda})
. Since
\alpha_{\Lambda}
involves
h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}
, Lemma
5.1
gives that
\beta
involves

(h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1})\circ(h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}})=h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}-1}.

At the same time,
\beta
satisfies condition (ii) in the statement of our claim by construction. It also satisfies condition (iii): Let
0\leq u<r
and
0\leq v<r^{\prime}
. Since
\beta
is essential,
\beta
does not involve
h^{u}\times h^{v}
. If it involved
l_{u}\times l_{v}
, then we would have that
(h^{u}\times h^{v})\beta=l_{0}\times l_{0}
, and so
X\times Y
would have a
0
-cycle of degree
1
. But then the same would be true of
X
and
Y
, contradicting the anisotropy of
\varphi
and
\psi
via Springer’s theorem. To complete the proof, it now only remains to show that
a\geq{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
and that
\beta
involves
l_{a}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}
. To see this, let
F_{s-1}
be the unique field in the nondefective splitting tower of
\varphi
with
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{F_{s-1}})}:={\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
. Overriding the notation used in the construction of
\beta
, let us now set
\varphi^{\prime}:=\varphi_{s-1}=(\varphi_{F_{s-1}})_{\mathrm{an}}
and
\psi^{\prime}:=(\psi_{F_{s-1}})_{\mathrm{an}}
. As before, set
X^{\prime}:=X_{\varphi^{\prime}}
and
Y^{\prime}:=X_{\psi^{\prime}}
. If needed, we choose orientations of
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
, and use these to orient
X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
. Let
p\colon R_{X\times Y}\rightarrow R_{X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}
be the composition of the scalar extension map
R_{X\times Y}\rightarrow R_{(X\times Y)_{F_{s-1}}}
and the map
f\colon R_{(X\times Y)_{F_{s-1}}}\rightarrow R_{X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}
of Proposition
6.2
. By the latter,
p(\beta)
is then an element of
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{X}+b}(X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime})
involving
h^{0}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}-1-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{s-1}})}}
. By Lemma
8.1
, there is an integer
0\leq t^{\prime}<t
such that for any separable extension
K/F_{s-1}
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi^{\prime}_{K})}>0
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi^{\prime}_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}(\varphi)}-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{s-1}})}
. Since
p(\beta)
involves
h^{0}\times l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}-1-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{s-1}})}}
, Lemma
8.2
then tells us that it also involves
l_{a^{\prime}}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}(\psi)}-{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F_{s-1}})}}
for some integer
a^{\prime}\geq 0
. In particular,
\beta
involves
l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+a^{\prime}}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}(\psi)}}
. Let
K
be the unique field in the nondefective splitting tower of
\psi
such that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}={\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}+1}(\psi)}
. Since
\beta
involves
l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+a^{\prime}}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}(\psi)}}
, Proposition
6.3
then implies that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+a^{\prime}
. Since
a^{\prime}>0
, our standing assumption on
\varphi
and
\psi
then tells us that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}
, and so
t^{\prime}=t-1
. Thus,
\beta
involves
l_{{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+a^{\prime}}\times h^{{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}}
. Since
\beta
has degree
d_{X}+b=d_{Y}-({\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}+a)
, we then have that
a={\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+a^{\prime}
. Since
a^{\prime}\geq 0
, this completes the proof. ∎

In the course of the proof, we showed that the integer
a:=d_{X}-d_{Y}-{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}+{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}+{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}-1
coincides with
{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}-1
under the standing hypotheses. In other words:

Corollary 8.4
.

Suppose there are integers
1\leq s\leq h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\varphi)
and
1\leq t\leq h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\psi)
such that for every separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}
. Then

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-{\mathfrak{j}_{s-1}(\varphi)}-{\mathfrak{j}_{s}(\varphi)}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi}-{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\psi)}-{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}.

Now, since
a(\Lambda^{U}(X))=0
, the
m=0
case of Theorem
8.3
gives:

Corollary 8.5
.

Suppose there exists an integer
0\leq n<r^{\prime}
such that for every separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>0
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>n
. Then
\Lambda^{U}(X)[n]
is an element of
\mathrm{MDT}(\psi)
.

In particular, we get our earlier claim (extending the stable birational invariance of the Izhboldin dimension):

Corollary 8.6
.

If
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
, then
\Lambda^{U}(X)=\Lambda^{U}(Y)
.

Proof.

In this case, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>0
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>0
for every separable extension
K/F
. By the previous corollary, it follows that
\Lambda^{U}(X)
is an element of
\mathrm{MDT}(\psi)
. Since
a(\Lambda^{U}(X))=0
, we then have that
\Lambda^{U}(Y)=\Lambda^{U}(X)
. ∎

Remarks 8.7

(Nondegenerate forms)
.

In the case where
\varphi
and
\psi
are nondegenerate, Theorem
8.3
gives the following stronger result: Suppose there are integers
0\leq m<r
and
0\leq n<r^{\prime}
such that for every separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>m
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>n
. If
M(X)
admits a direct summand
N
in
\mathrm{Chow}(F,\mathbb{F}_{2})
such that
a(N)=m
, then
M(Y)
admits a direct summand isomorphic to the Tate twist
N(n-m)
. This follows from Theorem
8.3
and the discussion of
[
3
, Ch. XVII]
. Similarly, Corollary
8.6
yields that when
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
, the upper motives of
X
and
Y
in
\mathrm{Chow}(F,\mathbb{F}_{2})
are isomorphic.

8.B.
Examples: Pfister Neighbours and Strongly Excellent Forms

Let
\varphi
be an anisotropic nonquasilinear quadratic form over
F
of type
(r,s)
. We set
X:=X_{\varphi}
,
h:=h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\varphi)
, and write
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}+m
for integers
n\geq 0
and
1\leq m\leq 2^{n}
.

Lemma 8.8
.

If
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour with ambient general Pfister form
\pi
and complementary form
\varphi^{c}
, then:

(1)

For every integer
0\leq i<m
and every separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\pi_{K})}>0
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>i
;

(2)

If
m<r
, then for every integer
m\leq i<r
and every separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi^{c}_{K})}>i-m
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>i
.

Proof.

(1) This holds since
\pi
and
\varphi
are stably birationally equivalent forms with maximal splitting (Lemma
2.11
).

(2) Recall that
\varphi^{c}
is an anisotropic form of dimension
2^{n}-m={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2m
satisfying
\varphi^{c}\sim\varphi\perp\pi
. Like
\varphi
,
\varphi^{c}
is dominated by
\pi
. In particular, if
K/F
is a separable extension such that either
\varphi_{K}
or
\varphi^{c}_{K}
is isotropic, then
\pi_{K}
is isotropic, and hence hyperbolic. Then
\varphi^{c}_{K}\sim\varphi_{K}
, and so
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi^{c}_{K})}={\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}-m
. The desired assertion therefore holds. ∎

Applying Theorem
8.3
, we get:

Theorem 8.9
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour with complementary form
\varphi^{c}
. Then
r\geq m
, and the following hold:

(1)

If
r=m
, then
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
consists of the sets
\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[i]
with
0\leq i<m
;

(2)

If
r>m
, then
\varphi^{c}
is a nonquasilinear nondefective form, and
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
consists of the sets
\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[i]
with
0\leq i<m
, together with the sets
\Lambda[m]
with
\Lambda\in\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.11
, we have

r\leq 2^{n}-s={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-m-s=(2r+s)-m-s=2r-m,

and so
r\geq m
. Let
\pi
be the ambient Pfister form of
\varphi
. Then
h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\pi)=1
(Lemma
7.19
), and so
\Lambda^{U}(X_{\pi})=\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}
by Proposition
7.18
. In view of Lemma
8.8
(1), Corollary
8.5
then implies that
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
contains the sets
\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[i]
with
0\leq i<m
. We now consider the two cases.

(1) If
r=m
, then the union of the elements of
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
found above is all of
\Lambda(X)
, and so
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
is as stated.

(2) If
r>m
, then Lemma
2.11
(3) gives that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=m<r
, and so
h\geq 2
. Moreover, we have
\varphi_{1}\simeq\varphi^{c}_{F(\varphi)}
by Lemma
2.11
(2), so
\varphi
is nonquasilinear and nondefective of type
(r-m,s)
. Now if
m\leq i<r
, then Lemma
8.8
(2) tells us that for every separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi^{c}_{K})}>i-m
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>i
. By Theorem
8.3
, it follows that
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
also contains sets
\Lambda[m]
with
\Lambda\in\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c})
. Since
|\Lambda(X_{\varphi^{c}})=2(r-m)=|\Lambda|-2m
, the union of these sets and the other elements of
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
found previously is all of
\Lambda(X)
, and so
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
is as stated. ∎

We can enhance this as follows. Recall that we write
\varphi_{1}
for the form
(\varphi_{F(\varphi)})_{\mathrm{an}}
. When
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour with complementary form
\varphi^{c}
, we have
\varphi_{1}\simeq(\varphi^{c})_{F(\varphi)}
by Lemma
2.11
(2). We now have:

Proposition 8.10
.

In the situation of Theorem
8.9

\mathrm{(2)}
, we have
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c})=\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi_{1})
. Thus,
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
consists of the sets
\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[i]
with
0\leq i<m
, together with the sets
\Lambda[m]
with
\Lambda\in\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi_{1})
.

Proof.

Set
Y:=X_{\varphi^{c}}
. Let
\pi
be the ambient general Pfister form of
\varphi
, and set
X:=X_{\pi}
. If
y\in Y\times Y
, then
Y(F(y))\neq\emptyset
, and so
\varphi^{c}_{F(y)}
is isotropic. Since
\pi
dominates
\varphi
, it follows that
\pi_{F(y)}
is isotropic, and hence hyperbolic. The canonical map
\mathrm{Ch}(X_{F(y)})\rightarrow R_{X_{F(y)}}
is then an isomorphism (see §
4.B
), so we may identify
\mathrm{Ch}(X_{F(y)})
with
R_{X_{F(y)}}
. Let
0\leq i\leq d_{Y}
. Since
d_{Y}=2^{n}-m-2<2^{n}-1=\frac{d_{X}}{2}
, the only non-zero element of
\mathrm{Ch}^{i}(X_{F(y)})
is
h^{i}
, which lies in the image of the scalar extension map
\mathrm{Ch}^{i}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ch}^{i}(X_{F(y)})
. By
[
3
, Lem. 88.5]
, it follows that the scalar extension map
\mathrm{Ch}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ch}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2}_{F(\pi)})
is surjective. The induced map
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2})\rightarrow\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d_{Y}}(Y^{2}_{F(\pi)})
is then an isomorphism, and so
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c})=\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c}_{F(\pi)})
. Now since
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\pi
, the fields
F(\varphi)
and
F(\pi)
admit a common extension which is purely transcendental over both. By Lemma
7.12
, we then have that

\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c})=\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c}_{F(\pi)})=\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi^{c}_{F(\varphi)})=\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi_{1}),

as desired. ∎

This yields:

Proposition 8.11
.

Suppose that
\varphi_{i}
is a Pfister neighbour for every
0\leq i<h
. Then there exist unique integers
n+1=n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{h}>\mathrm{log}_{2}(s)+1
such that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n_{1}}-2^{n_{2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h}s
. Moreover:

(1)

For each
0\leq i<h
, we have
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{i}}=2^{n_{i+1}}-2^{n_{i+2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1-i}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h-i}s
.

(2)

If we set
m_{i+1}:={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{i}}-2^{n_{i+1}-1}=2^{n_{i+1}-1}-2^{n_{i+2}}+2^{n_{i+3}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1-i}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h-i}s
for each
0\leq i<h
, then
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
consists of the sets

\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n_{i+1}-1}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[m_{1}+\cdots+m_{i}+j]

with
0\leq i<h
and
0\leq j<m_{i+1}
.

Proof.

Per the statement, let us set
n_{1}:=n+1
. The integer
m_{1}
in (2) is then equal to
m
, which equals
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
by Lemma
2.11
(3). By hypothesis,
\varphi
is a neighbour of an
n_{1}
-fold general Pfister form, and so
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{1}}=2^{n_{1}}-{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{1}}
. We now proceed by induction on
h
. If
h=1
, then
\varphi_{1}\simeq\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)_{F(\varphi)}
, and so
s<2^{n_{1}-1}
and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n_{1}}-s
. It is clear that
n_{1}
is the unique integer satisfying these conditions. Moreover, we have
r={\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=m
, and so Theorem
8.9
(1) gives that
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
consists of the sets
\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n_{1}-1}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[j]
with
0\leq j<m=m_{0}
. This proves the desired assertions in this case. Suppose now that
h\geq 2
. In this case,
r>{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=m
. By Theorem
8.9
and Proposition
8.10
,
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
then consists of the sets
\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n_{1}-1}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[j]
with
0\leq j<m=m_{1}
, together with the sets
\Lambda[m]=\Lambda[m_{1}]
with
\Lambda\in\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi_{1})
. Note, however, that
\varphi_{1}
is an anisotropic form of type
(r-m,s)
, nondefective height
h-1
and dimension
<2^{n_{1}}
satisfying the same condition as
\varphi
. By the induction hypothesis, it follows that there exist unique integers
n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{h}>\mathrm{log}_{2}(s)+1
such that:

•

For each
1\leq i<h
,
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{i}}=2^{n_{i+1}}-2^{n_{i+2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1-i}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h-i}s
;

•

If we set
m_{i+1}:={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{i}}-2^{n_{i+1}-1}=2^{n_{i+1}-1}-2^{n_{i+2}}+2^{n_{i+3}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1-i}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h-i}s
for each
1\leq i<h
, then
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi_{1})
consists of the sets

\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n_{i+1}-1}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[m_{1}+\cdots+m_{i}+j]

with
1\leq i<h
and
0\leq j<m_{i+1}
.

In view of the preceding remarks, the desired assertions then follow. ∎

By Lemmas
2.11
(2) and
2.12
, the hypothesis in the proposition is satisfied when
\varphi
is strongly excellent (see the discussion following Lemma
2.12
). Thus:

Corollary 8.12
.

If
\varphi
is strongly excellent, then there exist unique integers
n+1=n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{h}>\mathrm{log}_{2}(s)+1
such that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n_{1}}-2^{n_{2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h}s
. Moreover:

(1)

For each
0\leq i<h
, we have
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{i}}=2^{n_{i+1}}-2^{n_{i+2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1-i}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h-i}s
.

(2)

If we set
m_{i+1}:={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{i}}-2^{n_{i+1}-1}=2^{n_{i+1}-1}-2^{n_{i+2}}+2^{n_{i+3}}+\cdots+(-1)^{h-1-i}2^{n_{h}}+(-1)^{h-i}s
for each
0\leq i<h
, then
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
consists of the sets

\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n_{i+1}-1}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}[m_{1}+\cdots+m_{i}+j]

with
0\leq i<h
and
0\leq j<m_{i+1}
.

We expect that Proposition
8.11
says nothing more than this, i.e., that if
\varphi_{i}
is a Pfister neighbour for every
0\leq i<h
, then
\varphi
is strongly excellent. Proving this amounts to showing that if
h\geq 2
and both
\varphi
and
\varphi_{1}
are Pfister neighbours, then
\varphi^{c}
is also a Pfister neighbour. Specifically, what has to be shown here is that if
\pi
is the ambient general Pfister form of
\varphi_{1}
, then
\pi
descends from
F(\varphi)
to
F
. However, we currently do not know how to prove this, even in the case where
\varphi
is nondegenerate.

8.C.
Addendum: Virtual Pfister Neighbours

As in the previous subsection, let
\varphi
be a nonquasilinear anisotropic quadratic form over
F
of type
(r,s)
. Set
X:=X_{\varphi}
,
h=h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\varphi)
, and write
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}+m
for integers
n\geq 0
and
1\leq m\leq 2^{n}
.

If there exists an extension
K/F
such that
\varphi_{K}
is an anisotropic Pfister neighbour, then we say that
\varphi
is a
virtual Pfister neighbour
. Using the main result of the previous subsection, we get the following restriction on the MDT invariant for these forms:

Lemma 8.13
.

If
\varphi
is a virtual Pfister neighbour, then:

(1)

m
is in the nondefective splitting pattern of
\varphi
;

(2)

For each integer
0\leq i<m
,
i_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
(2^{n}+i-1)^{\mathrm{up}}
are connected in
\Lambda(X)
;

(3)

If
1\leq t\leq h
is such that
{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}=m
, then
{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\varphi)}_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda^{U}(X)
.

Proof.

(1) If
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour, then
m={\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
(Lemma
2.11
(3)). If
\varphi
is a virtual Pfister neighbour,
m
then lies in the nondefective splitting pattern of
\varphi
by Lemma
2.3
.

(2) If
K/F
is a field extension with
\mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{d}}(\varphi)=0
, then it is clear that any connections existing in
\Lambda(X_{K})
also exist in
\Lambda(X)
. To prove the assertion, we can therefore assume that
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour. But in this case, the claim holds by Theorem
8.9
.

(3) As
0_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda^{U}(X)
, we have
(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\in\Lambda^{U}(X)
by (2). Since
{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}=m={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2^{n}=d_{X}-2^{n}-2
, we then also have that
{\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}(\varphi)}_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda^{U}(X)
by Proposition
7.13
.
∎

It is an intriguing problem to determine sufficient conditions for an anisotropic quadratic form to be a virtual Pfister neighbour. While the nondefective spiltting pattern is unable to detect this property in general, we do have the following special cases:

Proposition 8.14
.

\varphi
is a virtual Pfister neighbour in the following cases:

(1)

\varphi
has maximal splitting, i.e,
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=m
;

(2)

m=1
;

(3)

m=2
and
2
lies in the nondefective splitting pattern of
\varphi
;

Note that case (2) has been treated in
[
7
, Prop. 3.1]
. A small modification of the argument gives the more general case (1). Over fields of characteristic not 2, the analogues of cases (1) and (3) are due to Hoffmann (
[
4
, Cor. 3]
) and Izhboldin (
[
8
, Thm. 5.8]
), respectively. The basis of the arguments (which goes back to
[
4
]
) is the following: Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n+1}
be indeterminates, and set
K:=F(T_{1},\ldots,T_{n+1})
. Consider the
(n+1)
-fold Pfister form
\pi:={\langle\!\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n+1}\rangle\!\rangle}_{b}
over
K
. It is straightforward to check that
\pi
is anisotropic, and showing that
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour amounts to showing that there exists an extension
L/K
such that
\pi_{L}
is anisotropic and dominates a nonzero scalar multiple of
\varphi_{L}
. We have the following lemma:

Lemma 8.15
.

In the above situation, set
\psi:=\varphi\perp\pi
, and let
K=K_{0}\subset K_{1}\subset\cdots\subset K_{h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\psi)}
be the nondefective splitting tower of
\psi
.

(1)

Let
i
be a nonnegative integer in the nondefective spitting pattern of
\varphi
. Then:

\mathrm{(i)}

There exists an integer
1\leq t<h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\psi)
such that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K_{t}})}=i
and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{t}}=2^{n}+m-2i
;

\mathrm{(ii)}

If
i\geq m
, then there exists a separable extension
L/K
such that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{L})}=i
and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\psi_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}}=2^{n}-m+2i
.

(2)

Let
1\leq t<h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\psi)
be such that
\varphi_{K_{t}}
is anisotropic. Suppose that one of the following holds:

\mathrm{(i)}

\pi_{K_{t+1}}
is isotropic;

\mathrm{(ii)}

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;((\psi_{t})_{K_{t}(\varphi)})_{\mathrm{an}}}<2^{n}+m-2{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
;

\mathrm{(iii)}

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\varphi_{K_{t+1}})_{\mathrm{an}}}<2^{n+1}-{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{t+1}}
.

Then
\varphi_{K_{t}}\prec\pi_{K_{t}}
, and so
\varphi_{K_{t}}
is a Pfister neighbour.

Proof.

(1) Since
\pi_{K(\pi)}
is hyperbolic, we have
\psi_{K(\pi)}\sim\varphi_{K(\pi)}
. But it is straightforward to see that
K(\pi)
is a purely transcendental extension of
F
, and so (i) holds by Lemma
2.3
. Suppose now that
i\geq m
. By Lemma
2.3
, there exists a separable extension
F^{\prime}/F
such that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{F^{\prime}})}=i
. Set
\varphi^{\prime}:=(\varphi_{F^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{an}}
. Then
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi^{\prime}}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2i=2^{n}+m-2i<2^{n}
. By
[
7
, Prop. 3.1]
, it follows that exists a separable extension
L
of
F^{\prime}(T_{1},\ldots,T_{n+1})=K\cdot F^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{L}
is anisotropic and
\varphi^{\prime}_{L}\prec\pi_{L}
. Then
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{L})}=i
, and we have

\psi_{L}\sim(\varphi\perp\pi)_{L}\sim\varphi^{\prime}_{L}\perp\pi_{L}\sim(\varphi^{\prime}_{L})_{\pi_{L}}^{c}.

Since
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\varphi^{\prime}_{L})_{\pi_{L}}^{c}}=2^{n+1}-{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi^{\prime}}=2^{n}+m-2i
, it follows that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\psi_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}}=2^{n}-m+2i
, proving the claim (note that
L/F
is separable, being a tower of separable extensions).

(2) By definition, we have
\psi_{t}\sim\varphi_{K_{t}}\perp\pi_{K_{t}}
. Let us now consider the three cases of interest.

Suppose first that we are in case (i), and let
j\leq t
be the largest integer for which
\pi_{K_{j}}
is anisotropic. By Lemma
2.1
, we have

\psi_{j}\perp\pi_{K_{j}}\sim\varphi_{K_{j}}\perp\pi_{K_{j}}\perp\pi_{K_{j}}\sim\varphi_{K_{j}}.

Since
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi_{K_{j}}}=2^{n}+m<{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\psi_{j}\perp\pi_{K_{j}})}
, and since
\pi_{K_{j}}
is anisotropic,
\pi_{K_{j}}
and
\pi_{K_{j}}
represent a common nonzero value of
K_{j}
. Since
\pi_{K_{j+1}}
is isotropic, and hence hyperbolic, the Cassels-Pfister subform theorem (
[
3
, Thm. 22.5]
), then gives that
\psi_{j}\prec\pi_{K_{j}}
. By Lemma
2.1
, we then have that

\varphi_{K_{j}}\sim\varphi_{K_{j}}\perp\pi_{K_{j}}\perp\pi_{K_{j}}\sim\psi_{j}\perp\pi_{K_{j}}\sim(\psi_{j})_{\pi_{K_{j}}}^{c},

and so
\varphi_{K_{j}}\simeq(\psi_{j})_{\pi_{K_{j}}}^{c}\prec\pi_{K_{j}}
(both forms being anisotropic). In particular,
\varphi_{K_{t}}\prec\pi_{K_{t}}
.

Suppose now that we are in case (ii) or (iii). Let
L
be
K_{t}(\varphi)
or
K_{t+1}
, depending on whether we are in the former or latter cases, and set
\varphi^{\prime}:=(\varphi_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}
and
\psi^{\prime}:=(\psi_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}=((\psi_{t})_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}
. Then
\psi^{\prime}\sim\varphi^{\prime}\perp\pi_{L}
, and the dimension hypothesis in each case tells us that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi^{\prime}}+{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi^{\prime}}<2^{n+1}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\pi}
. Since
L/K
is separable, Lemma
7.17
then gives that

{\mathfrak{i}_{0}(\varphi^{\prime}\perp\pi_{L})}={\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi^{\prime}\perp\pi_{L})}=\frac{{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi^{\prime}}+{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\pi}-{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi^{\prime}}}{2}>{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi^{\prime}}.

If
\pi_{L}
were anisotropic, it would then follow from
[
6
, Prop. 3.11]
that
\varphi^{\prime}\prec\pi_{L}
. But we would then have that
\psi^{\prime}\sim\varphi^{\prime}\perp\pi_{L}\sim\varphi^{\prime}_{\pi_{L}}
, giving that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi^{\prime}}=2^{n+1}-{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi^{\prime}}
, a contradiction. Thus,
\pi_{L}
is isotropic, and hence hyperbolic. If
L=K_{t+1}
, we are then back in case (i). Suppose therefore that
L=K_{t}(\varphi)
. Again, if
\pi_{K_{t+1}}
is isotropic, then we are in case (i), so we may assume otherwise. In particular, we may assume that
\pi_{K_{t}}
is anisotropic. Now since
t\geq 1
,
\varphi_{K_{t}}\perp\pi_{K_{t}}=\psi_{K_{t}}
is isotropic. Since both
\varphi_{K_{t}}
and
\pi_{K_{t}}
are anisotropic, they then represent a common element of
K_{t}^{\times}
. Since
\pi_{L}
is hyperbolic, another application of the Cassels-Pfister subform theorem then gives that
\varphi_{K_{t}}\prec\pi_{K_{t}}
, as desired. ∎

We can now prove Proposition
8.14
:

Proof of Proposition
8.14
.

As in Lemma
8.15
, set
\psi:=\varphi\perp\pi
, and let
K=K_{0}\subset K_{1}\subset\cdots\subset K_{h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\psi)}
be the nondefective splitting tower of
\psi
. By part (1)(i) of the lemma (with
i=0
) there exists an integer
1\leq j<h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\psi)
such that
\varphi_{K_{j}}
is anisotropic and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j}}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}+m
. Now in all cases we are considering,
m
lies in the nondefective splitting pattern of
\varphi
. By part (1)(ii) of the lemma, it follows that there exists an extension
L/K
such that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{L})}=m
and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\psi_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}}=2^{n}+m
. By repeated application of Lemma
2.2
(2), the compositum
L\cdot K_{j}
is then a purely transcendental extension of
L
. In particular,
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\psi_{L\cdot K_{j}})_{\mathrm{an}}}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\psi_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}}=2^{n}+m={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j}}
, and so
\psi_{j}
remains anisotropic over
L\cdot K_{j}
. Since
\varphi_{L\cdot K_{j}}
is isotropic, it follows that
\varphi_{K_{j}}
and
\psi_{j}
are not stably birationally equivalent. At the same time, part (1)(i) of Lemma
8.15
also tells us that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi_{j}}\geq{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}\geq{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi_{K_{j}}}
. Since
\varphi_{K_{j}}
and
\psi_{j}
are not stably birationally equivalent, Theorems
2.7
and
2.9
then imply that
\varphi_{K_{j+1}}
is anisotropic. Since
\psi_{j+1}\sim\varphi_{K_{j+1}}\perp\pi_{K_{j+1}}
, and since
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j+1}}<{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j}}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}
, it follows that
\pi_{K_{j+1}}
is not hyperbolic, and is hence also anisotropic. There are now two cases to consider.

Case 1.

\varphi
has maximal splitting. In this case, we have

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\psi_{K_{j+1}(\varphi)})_{\mathrm{an}}}\leq{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j+1}}<{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j}}=2^{n}+m=2^{n}-m+2{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}.

Since
\varphi_{K_{t+1}}
is anisotropic, Lemma
8.15
(2) (case (ii)) then tells us that it is a Pfister neighbour, and so
\varphi
is a virtual Pfister neighbour.

Case 2.

m=2
and
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=1
. In this case, part (1)(i) of Lemma
8.15
shows that
j\leq h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\psi)-2
, and that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j+1}}=2^{n}
and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j+2}}=2^{n}-2<2^{n}+m-2{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
. If
\varphi_{K_{j+2}}
is anisotropic then Lemma
8.15
(2) (case (ii) with
t=j+2
) tells us that it is a Pfister neighbour. On the other hand, if
\varphi_{K_{j+2}}
is isotropic, then

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;(\varphi_{K_{j+2}})_{\mathrm{an}}}<{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}+2=2^{n+1}-(2^{n}-2)=2^{n+1}-{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{j+2}},

and so the same lemma (now case (iii) with
t=j+1
) then tells us that
\varphi_{K_{j+1}}
is a Pfister neighbour. Thus, in this case, either
\varphi_{K_{j+1}}
or
\varphi_{K_{j+2}}
is an anisotropic Pfister neighbour. Either way,
\varphi
is a virtual Pfister neighbour, and so the result holds. ∎

9.
The Degenerate Pfister Neighbour Problem

In this section we fix an anisotropic quadratic form
\varphi
of type
(r,s)
over
F
. We assume that
r\geq 1
(i.e., that
\varphi
is not quasilinear) and set
X:=X_{\varphi}
. We also write
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}+m
for integers
n\geq 0
and
1\leq m\leq 2^{n}
. Our goal is to investigate the conditions under which
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour. By Lemma
2.11
(1), a necessary condition is that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, and so we assume that this is satisfied in everything that follows.

Now, if
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour, then
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
. More precisely, we have
\varphi_{1}\simeq(\varphi^{c})_{F(\varphi)}
, where
\varphi^{c}
is the complementary form of
\varphi
in its ambient general Pfister form (Lemma
2.11
(2)). For nondegenerate forms, a well-known result (essentially due to Knebusch) asserts that the converse holds: If
\varphi
is nondegenerate and
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
, then
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour (
[
3
, Thm. 28.1]
). If we relax the nondegeneracy assumption, however, then this is no longer true in general. For instance, if
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
, then
\varphi_{1}\simeq\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)_{F(\varphi)}
(Lemma
7.17
), but
\varphi
need not be a Pfister neighbour. Simple examples may be constructed as follows:

Example 9.1
.

Suppose that
r=1
. Since
r+s\leq 2^{n}
and
2r+s={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}>2^{n}
, we then have that
s=2^{n}-1
. Now, since
r=1
, we automatically have that
h_{\mathrm{nd}}=1
(Lemma
7.17
). However,
\varphi
need not be a Pfister neighbour in this case. For example, let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{2^{n}}
be indeterminates, and let
\sigma
be the form
[1,X_{1}]\perp\langle X_{2},\ldots,X_{2^{n}}\rangle
of type
(1,2^{n}-1)
over the rational function field
\mathbb{F}_{2}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2^{n}})
. It is straightforward to see that
\sigma
is anisotropic. If
n\geq 2
, however, then
\sigma
is not a Pfister neighbour. Indeed, suppose that
\sigma
were a neighbour of an anisotropic
(n+1)
-fold Pfister form
\pi
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2^{n}})
. The complementary form
\sigma^{c}
is then
\langle X_{2},\ldots,X_{2^{n}}\rangle
. Over
K:=\mathbb{F}_{2}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2^{n}})(\sqrt{X_{2}X_{3}})
,
\sigma^{c}
is isotropic, so
\pi_{K}
is isotropic and hence hyperbolic. Then
\sigma_{K}\sim(\sigma\perp\pi)_{K}\sim\sigma^{c}_{K}
. Since
\sigma^{c}_{K}
is isotropic, it follows that
{\mathfrak{i}_{0}(\varphi_{K})}\geq 2
. But it is again straightforward to check that
[1,X_{1}]\perp\langle X_{3},\ldots,X_{2^{n}}\rangle
remains anisotropic over
K
. Since this is a codimension-1 subform of
\sigma
, we cannot have that
{\mathfrak{i}_{0}(\sigma_{K})}\geq 2
, and so
\sigma
is not a Pfister neighbour.

Nevertheless, it has been conjectured by Hoffmann and Laghribi that the such examples cannot arise when
s
is sufficiently small relative to
r
:

Conjecture 9.2

(
[
6
, Conj. 6.5]
)
.

Suppose that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, and that
s<2r
. If
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
, then
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour.

At present, this has only been established in the case where
s\leq 4
(
[
6
, Thm. 6.6]
). Note that since
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, the inequality
s<2r
implies that
s<\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}
. We expect that the latter condition is in fact sufficient:

Conjecture 9.3
.

Suppose that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, and that
s<\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}
. If
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
, then
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour.

We provide in this section some evidence for this conjecture. The basic result is the following, which shows that when
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
,
\varphi
exhibits key behaviour expected of a Pfister neighbour (see Lemma
2.11
and Theorem
8.9
, in particular):

Theorem 9.4
.

Suppose that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, and that
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
. Then:

(1)

\varphi
has maximal splitting;

(2)

\varphi
is a virtual Pfister neighbour;

(3)

\Lambda^{U}(X)=\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}
;

(4)

There exists an anisotropic form
\psi
of type
(2^{n}-s,s)
over
F
such that
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
. Moreover, if
h_{\mathrm{nd}}=1
, then
\psi
may be taken to be
\varphi
itself, i.e.,
r=2^{n}-s
.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be a form over
F
with
\varphi_{1}\simeq\tau_{F(\varphi)}
. Since
\varphi_{F(\varphi)}
is nondefective, we have

\mathrm{ql}(\tau)_{F(\varphi)}\simeq\mathrm{ql}(\tau_{F(\varphi)})\simeq\mathrm{ql}(\varphi_{1})\simeq\mathrm{ql}(\varphi_{F(\varphi)})\simeq\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)_{F(\varphi)}.

By Lemma
2.1
, we then have that
{\mathfrak{i}_{0}((\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)\perp\mathrm{ql}(\psi))_{F(\varphi)})}=s
. Since
F(\varphi)/F
is separable, however, this implies that
{\mathfrak{i}_{0}(\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)\perp\mathrm{ql}(\psi))}=s
, and so
\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)\simeq\mathrm{ql}(\psi)
by another application of Lemma
2.1
. Let
\varphi^{\prime}
(resp.
\tau^{\prime}
) be a nondegenerate form of dimension
2r
(resp.
2r-2{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)})
) over
F
such that
\varphi\simeq\varphi^{\prime}\perp\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)
(resp.
\tau\simeq\tau^{\prime}\perp\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)
). Set
\psi:=\varphi^{\prime}\perp\tau^{\prime}\perp\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)
. By definition,
\psi
has type
(2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)},s)
. Moreover, if
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
, then
\tau^{\prime}=0
, and so
\psi=\varphi
. We state the following claim:

Claim.
For any separable extension
K/F
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>0
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-1
.

Before proving the claim, let us first use it to complete the proof of the proposition. Set
i:={\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi)}
. If
i
were equal to
2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
, then we would have that
\psi\sim\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)
. By Lemma
2.1
, however, this would give that

\varphi\sim\varphi\perp\tau^{\prime}\perp\tau^{\prime}\simeq\psi\perp\tau^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)\perp\tau^{\prime}\simeq\tau,

contradicting the anisotropy of
\varphi
(note that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\tau}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}<{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}
). We therefore have that
i<2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
, and so
\psi^{\prime}:=\psi_{\mathrm{an}}
is a nondefective and nonquasilinear form of type
(2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i,s)
. If
K/F
is a separable extension, then the claim tells us that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>0
if and only if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi^{\prime}_{K})}>2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i-1
. By Corollary
8.5
, it follows that
\Lambda:=\Lambda^{U}(X)[2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i-1]
is an element of
\mathrm{MDT}(\psi^{\prime})
. Let
h
be the nondefective height of
\psi^{\prime}
. Since
\psi^{\prime}
has type
(2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i,s)
, and since
a(\Lambda)=2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i-1
, Proposition
7.14
and Corollary
7.15
imply that
\Lambda=\{(2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i-1)_{\mathrm{lo}},({\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi^{\prime}}-2-{\mathfrak{j}_{h-1}(\psi^{\prime})})^{\mathrm{up}}\}
. Since
b(\Lambda^{U}(X))={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}-1
(Proposition
7.16
), it then follows that

({\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}-1)+(2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i-1)={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi^{\prime}}-2-{\mathfrak{j}_{h-1}(\psi^{\prime})}.

Since
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2r+s-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}
and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi^{\prime}}=2(2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-i-1)+s
, this amounts to the equality
i=-{\mathfrak{j}_{h-1}(\psi^{\prime})}
, and so
i={\mathfrak{j}_{h-1}(\psi^{\prime})}=0
. In other words,
\psi
is anisotropic of nondefective height
1
. By the claim, we then have that
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
. In particular,
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}\geq 2^{n}
(Theorem
2.9
, Corollary
2.6
). On the other hand, since
\psi
has nondefective height
1
, we have
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi_{1}}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\mathrm{ql}(\psi)}=s<2^{n}
(Lemma
7.17
). By Theorem
2.5
(Karpenko’s theorem on the values of
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
), we must then have that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}=2^{n}
. By the preceding remarks, we then also have that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}
, and so (1) holds. In view of Proposition
8.14
, this also gives (2). For (3), we have already seen above that
|\Lambda^{U}(X)|=|\Lambda|=2
. By Proposition
7.16
, however, we then have that
\Lambda^{U}(X)=\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},({\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}=\{0_{\mathrm{lo}},(2^{n}-1)^{\mathrm{up}}\}
, and so the desired assertion holds. It now only remains to prove (4). Since we have already shown that
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
, we just have to show that
\psi
has type
(2^{n}-s,s)
, i.e., that
2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=2^{n}-s
. But since
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2r+s
, this is simply a reformulation of the fact that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}
.

We now complete the proof by proving the claim. Let
K/F
be a separable extension. Suppose first that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-1
. Since
\psi
has type
(2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)},s)
, we then have that
\psi_{K}\sim\mathrm{ql}(\psi)_{K}=\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)_{K}
. By Lemma
2.1
, we then have that

\varphi_{K}\sim(\varphi\perp\mathrm{ql}(\varphi))_{K}\sim(\varphi\perp\psi)_{K}\sim(\tau^{\prime}\perp\mathrm{ql}(\varphi))_{K}\simeq\tau_{K},

and so
\varphi_{K}
is isotropic (again, we have
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\tau}<{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}
). Since
K/F
is separable, we then have that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>0
(Lemma
2.3
). Conversely, if
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\varphi_{K})}>0
, then the extension
K(\varphi)/K
is purely transcendental by Lemma
2.2
(2). In particular, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}={\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K(\varphi)})}\geq{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F(\varphi)})}
. But since
\psi=\varphi^{\prime}\perp\tau
, we have

\psi_{F(\varphi)}\simeq\varphi^{\prime}_{F(\varphi)}\perp\tau_{F(\varphi)}\simeq\varphi^{\prime}_{F(\varphi)}\perp\varphi_{1}\sim(\varphi^{\prime}\perp\varphi)_{F(\varphi)}\sim(\varphi^{\prime}\perp\varphi^{\prime}\perp\mathrm{ql}(\varphi))_{F(\varphi)}\sim\mathrm{ql}(\varphi)_{F(\varphi)}

by Lemma
2.1
, and so
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{F}(\varphi))}>2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-1
. In particular,
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\psi_{K})}>2r-{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}-1
, and so the claim holds. ∎

Note, in particular, that we have the following dimension restriction on the forms of nondefective height
1
(under our assumption that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
):

Corollary 9.5
.

Suppose that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
. If
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
, then
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n+1}-s
.

Proof.

In this case, part (4) of Theorem
9.4
tells us that
r=2^{n}-s
, and so
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2r+s=2(2^{n}-s)+s=2^{n+1}-s
.
∎

As remarked above,
\varphi_{1}
is trivially defined over
F
in the case where
h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\varphi)=1
. In particular, Conjecture
9.3
includes the following as a special case:

Conjecture 9.6
.

Suppose that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, and that
s<\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}
. If
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
, then is a Pfister neighbour.

Recall that we use the term
close Pfister neighbour
for a Pfister neighbour of nondefective height 1. If
\varphi
is a close Pfister neighbour, then
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n+1}-s
by Lemma
7.19
. Thus, Corollary
9.5
confirms the dimension part of Conjecture
9.6
. Moreover, as far as Conjecture
9.3
goes, Theorem
9.4
gives the following:

Corollary 9.7
.

Conjectures
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.6
are equivalent.

Proof.

We have already noted that Conjecture
9.3
implies
9.2
.

Conjecture
9.2

\Rightarrow
Conjecture
9.6
: Suppose that
s<\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}
. If
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
, then
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
(see the preceding discussion). Moreover, Corollary
9.5
tells us that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n+1}-s
, and so

2r={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-s=2^{n+1}-2s>2^{n+1}-2\left(\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}\right)=\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}>s.

By Conjecture
9.2
,
\varphi
is then a Pfister neighbour.

Conjecture
9.6

\Rightarrow
Conjecture
9.3
: Suppose that
s<\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}
and that
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
. By Theorem
9.4
,
\varphi
has maximal splitting, and there exists an anisotropic form
\psi
of type
(2^{n}-s,s)
over
F
such that
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
. By Theorem
2.9
(or Corollary
8.5
), we then have that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}
, and so
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\psi)}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi}-2^{n}=2(2^{n}-s)+s-2^{n}=2^{n}-s
. By Lemma
7.17
, this means that
\psi
has nondefective height
1
. Since
s<\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}
, Conjecture
9.6
then implies that
\psi
is a Pfister neighbour. But since
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
, the same is then true of
\varphi
, and so the claim holds. ∎

If we impose a slightly stronger assumption on
s
, then we can also relate Conjecture
9.3
to a well-known conjecture on nondegenerate forms. In fact, we state here two conjectures, the first of which is (essentially) due to Vishik, and the second of which is (essentially) due to Hoffmann (note that the
r+s\leq 2^{n}
condition is vacuously satisfied here):

Conjectures 9.8
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is nondegenerate.

(1)

If
|\Lambda^{U}(X)|=2
, then
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour.

(2)

If
\varphi
has maximal splitting and
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}>2^{n}+2^{n-2}
, then
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour.

Note that in (2), the standing hypotheses imply that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}>{\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\varphi)}
for all
1\leq t<h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\varphi)
. It then follows from Proposition
7.14
(2) that
|\Lambda^{U}(X)|=2
, and so (2) is in fact implied by (1). Nevertheless, one may hope that a more direct approach to (2) is achievable, and so we include it in our discussion. Using Theorem
9.4
, we can now show the following:

Proposition 9.9
.

Suppose that
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, and that
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
. Then
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour in the following cases:

(1)

s\leq 2^{n-1}
and Conjecture
9.8

\mathrm{(1)}
holds;

(2)

s\leq 2^{n-1}-2^{n-3}
and Conjecture
9.8

\mathrm{(2)}
holds.

Proof.

We may assume that
s\leq 2^{n-1}
. By Theorem
9.4
(4), there exists an anisotropic form
\psi
of type
(2^{n}-s,s)
over
F
such that
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
. Per the proof of Corollary
9.7
,
\psi
has nondefective height
1
, and so
\psi_{1}
is defined over
F
. Moreover, since
r+s\leq 2^{n}
, we have
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2r+s=2(r+s)-s\leq 2^{n+1}-s={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi}
. Since
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour if and only if
\psi
is, we can then replace
\varphi
with
\psi
in order to reduce to the case where
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n+1}-s
. By Theorem
9.4
(1), we then have that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2^{n}=2^{n}-s\geq s
. Let
\tau
be a nondegenerate subform of codimension
s-1
in
\varphi
. Since
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}\geq s
,
\tau_{F(\varphi)}
is isotropic and so
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\tau
. To prove that
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour in cases (1) and (2), it then suffices to show that the same is true of
\tau
.

(1) Since
\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\varphi
, we have
\Lambda^{U}(X_{\tau})=\Lambda^{U}(X)
by Corollary
8.6
. But since
\varphi_{1}
is defined over
F
, Theorem
9.4
(3) then tells us that
|\Lambda^{U}(X_{\tau})|=|\Lambda^{U}(X)|=2
. Since
\tau
is nondegenerate, it is then a Pfister neighbour by our hypothesis.

(2) Since
\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\varphi
, we have
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\tau}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}
by Theorem
2.9
(or Corollary
8.6
). In other words,
\tau
has maximal splitting. At the same time, we also have that

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\tau}>{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-s=2^{n+1}-2s\geq 2^{n+1}-(2^{n-1}-2^{n-3})=2^{n}+2^{n-2}.

Since
\tau
is nondegenerate, it is then a Pfister neighbour by our hypothesis. ∎

10.
Excellent Connections and the First Higher Isotropy Index

In this last section, we fix an anisotropic quadratic form
\varphi
of type
(r,s)
over
F
. We assume that
r\geq 1
(i.e., that
\varphi
is not quasilinear), and set
h:=h_{\mathrm{nd}}(\varphi)
and
X:=X_{\varphi}
. For ease of notation, we also set
\mathfrak{j}_{t}:={\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\varphi)}
and
\mathfrak{i}_{t}:={\mathfrak{i}_{t}(\varphi)}
for all
1\leq t\leq h
.

10.A.
Excellent Pairs

Let
j
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}
be the unique nonnegative integers for which
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n_{1}}-2^{n_{2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{j-1}2^{n_{j}}
and
n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{j-1}>n_{j}+1
. For each integer
1\leq i\leq j
, set

m_{i}:=2^{n_{i}-1}-2^{n_{i+1}}+\cdots+(-1)^{j-i}2^{n_{j}}.

Let
j^{\prime}
be
j
or
j-1
depending on whether
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}
is even or odd. Accounting for Remark
7.9
(2), the following definition is taken from
[
17
, §3]
:

Definition 10.1
.

Let
a,b
be integers with
0\leq a,d_{X}-b<r
. We say that the pair
(a,b)
is
excellent
(for
\varphi
) if there exists an integer
1\leq k\leq j^{\prime}
such that the following hold:

(1)

b-a=2^{n_{k}-1}-1
;

(2)

\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}m_{i}\leq a,d_{X}-b<\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}
.

The following lemma, which is a translation of Corollary
8.12
, explains the terminology:

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
a
and
b
be integers with
0\leq a,d_{X}-b<r
. If
\varphi
is strongly excellent, then the pair
(a,b)
is excellent if and only if
a_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
b^{\mathrm{up}}
are connected in
\Lambda(X)
.

10.B.
Excellent Connections for Nondegenerate Forms

In the situation of Lemma
10.2
, one may whether the nessecity part of the statement remains valid if we relax the assumption that
\varphi
be strongly excellent. Over fields of characteristic different from
2
, the analogous problem was shown to have a positive answer by Vishik in
[
17
, Thm. 1.3]
. Aside from the fact that anisotropic forms are nondegenerate in that setting, the only other reason for the characteristic restriction in the latter was the use of Brosnan’s Steenrod operations for Chow groups modulo
2
. Thanks to
[
11
]
, exactly the same arguments now go through for nondegenerate forms in characteristic
2
, and so we have:

Theorem 10.3
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is nondegenerate, and let
a
and
b
be integers with
0\leq a,d_{X}-b<r
. If the pair
(a,b)
is excellent, then
a_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
b^{\mathrm{up}}
are connected in
\Lambda(X)
.

Theorem
10.3
is perhaps the most profound known result concerning the MDT invariant. As shown by Vishik, it has a number of deep implications, including the following:

Theorem 10.4
.

Let
1\leq t<h
. Suppose that
\varphi
is nondegenerate, and that
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
admits an element
\Lambda
with
a(\Lambda)=\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}
. Let
j
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}
be the unique nonnegative integers for which
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}-\mathfrak{j}_{t}=2^{n_{1}}-2^{n_{2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{j-1}2^{n_{j}}
and
n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{j-1}>n_{j}+1
. For each integer
1\leq k\leq j
, consider the integer

d_{k}:=\left(\frac{{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}-\mathfrak{j}_{t}}{2}\right)+\sum_{i=k}^{k}(-1)^{k+i-1}2^{n_{i}-1}.

Then
(d_{k})_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda
. Moreover, if
t\leq t^{\prime}\leq h
is such that
\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}-1}<d_{k}\leq\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}
, then
d_{k}+\mathfrak{i}_{t}\leq\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The second statement follows from the first and Proposition
7.14
(2). It therefore suffices to prove the first statement. In the notation of
[
17
]
, the element
\Lambda\in\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
gives an indecomposable direct summand
N
of
M(X)
in
\mathrm{Chow}(F,\mathbb{F}_{2})
with
a(N)=\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}
(see Remark
7.9
(1)). The integer
d_{X}-2\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}-\mathfrak{j}_{t}
is then equal to
\mathrm{dim}(N)+1
, and the claim is equivalent to the second statement in
[
17
, Thm. 2.1]
(formulated in our setting). Given Theorem
10.3
, however, the proof of the latter goes through in characteristic
2
: The auxiliary roles played by
[
17
, Prop. 1.1]
and
[
17
, Obs. 2.3]
are assumed here by Proposition
7.13
and Lemma
7.10
, and the assertion of
[
17
, Lem. 2.2]
remains valid in light of Corollary
8.6
. With these remarks, the proof then goes through verbatim. ∎

We expect that Theorem
10.3
remains valid if we relax the requirement that
\varphi
be nondegenerate. Before discussing this and its implications, we first give some applications of Theorem
10.4
to problems in the degenerate setting.

10.C.
Forms of Nondefective Height 1 Revisited

Let
n
be the unique integer for which
2^{n}<{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}\leq 2^{n+1}
. In this subsection, we return to the case where
h=1
, i.e., where
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
. If
r+s\leq 2^{n}
(equivalently,
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}\leq 2^{n+1}-s
) and
s<\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}
, then Conjecture
9.6
predicts that
\varphi
is a (close) Pfister neighbour in this case. With a slight weakning of the second condition, the first condition may in fact be removed:

Proposition 10.5
.

Suppose that
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
. If
s\leq\frac{{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}}{3}
, then
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n+1}-s
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2r+s
, the assumption on
s
tells us that
r\geq s
. Since
h=1
, we then have that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=r\leq s
(Lemma
7.17
). Let
\psi
be a form of type
(r,1)
dominated by
\varphi
. Since
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\geq s
, we have that
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
(Lemma
2.10
). In particular, we have
\Lambda^{U}(X)=\Lambda^{U}(X_{\psi})
(Corollary
8.6
). Since
h=1
, Proposition
7.18
then gives that
|\Lambda^{U}(X_{\psi})|=|\Lambda^{U}(X)|=2
. Since
\psi
is nondegenerate, Theorem
10.4
then tells us that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}
is a power of
2
. But since
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
, Theorem
2.9
then gives that

r+s={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-r={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-\mathfrak{i}_{1}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}

is a power of
2
. By Corollary
2.6
, we then have that
r+s=2^{n}
, and so
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2(r+s)-s=2^{n+1}-s
, as claimed. ∎

In view of the discussion of the previous section, we therefore make the following conjecture on the classification of forms of nondefective height
1
:

Conjecture 10.6
.

If
\varphi
has nondefective height
1
, and
s\leq\frac{{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}}{3}
, then
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour.

For larger values of
s
, it is less clear what can be expected.

10.D.
The Values of the First Higher Isotropy Index

In this subsection, we consider the possible values of the integer
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
. Recall from Theorem
2.5
that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
is at most the largest
2
-power divisor of
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-\mathfrak{i}_{1}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}
. If we fix the value of
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}
and take no further information into account, then this result cannot be bettered: Given integers
d\geq
and
i\geq 1
such that
i
is at most the largest power of
2
that divides
d-i
, there exist an extension
K/F
and an anisotropic quadratic form
\psi
over
K
such that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi}=d
and
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\psi)}=i
. If we take into account the type
(r,s)
, however, then the situation already changes. First, the standard construction that demonstrates the optimality of the previous assertion (cf.
[
3
, Thm. 79.9]
) yields only the following:

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
v
be a nonnegative integer, and let
x
be the unique integer for which
x2^{v}<r\leq(x+1)2^{v}
. Suppose that
i
is a positive integer satisfying the following conditions:

\mathrm{(i)}

2r+s-i
is divisible by
2^{v}
;

\mathrm{(ii)}

i\leq r-x2^{v}
;

Then there exist an extension
K/F
anisotropic quadratic form
\psi
of type
(r,s)
over
K
such that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\psi)}=i
. Moreover, we have
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}=2r+s-i=y2^{v}
for some integer
y\geq 2x+1
.

Proof.

By (i), there exists an integer
y
such that
2r+s-i=y2^{v}
. By (ii), we have

y2^{v}=2r+s-i\geq 2r+s-(r-x2^{v})=r+s+x2^{v}>2x2^{v},

and so
y\geq 2x+1
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{v}Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{2x+2},Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{y-(2x+1)}
be indeterminates, and set
K:=F(X_{1},\ldots,Z_{y-(2x+1)})
. Over
K
, we may consider the form
\tau:=\mathfrak{b}\otimes q
, where
\mathfrak{b}:={\langle\!\langle X_{1},\ldots,X_{v}\rangle\!\rangle}_{b}
and
q:=Y_{1}[1,Y_{2}]\perp\cdots\perp Y_{2x+1}[1,Y_{2x+2}]\perp\langle Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{y-(2x+1)}\rangle
. We claim that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\tau)}=2^{v}
. Consider the field
L:=K[T]/(T^{2}+T+Y_{2})
. A routine computation (using the rationality of
L/F(X_{1},\ldots,\hat{Y_{2}},\ldots,Z_{y-(2x+1)})
, where the hat indicates omission) shows that
{\mathfrak{i}_{W}(\tau_{L})}=2^{v}
. Since
L/K
is separable, Lemma
2.3
then gives that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\tau)}\leq 2^{v}
. On the other hand, if
L/K
is any other separable extension, then
\mathfrak{b}_{L}
is anisotropic (anisotropic quasilinear quadratic forms remain anisotropic under separable extensions) and is still a divisor of
(\tau_{L})_{\mathrm{an}}
(a routine consequence of the roundness of bilinear and quadratic Pfister forms). We therefore have that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}\geq 2^{v}
, whence the claim. Observe now that
\tau
has dimension
(y+1)2^{v}
and type
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
, where
r^{\prime}=(x+1)2^{v},
and
s^{\prime}=(y-(2x+1))2^{v}
. Since

\displaystyle s\quad

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle y2^{v}+i-2r

\displaystyle\leq\quad

\displaystyle y2^{v}+(r-x2^{v})-2r

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(y-x)2^{v}-r

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\left((x+1)2^{v}-r\right)+\left(y-(2x+1)\right)2^{v}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(r^{\prime}-r)+s^{\prime},

\tau
therefore admits a subform
\psi
of type
(r,s)
. Then

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi}=2r+s=y2^{v}+i=(y+1)2^{v}-(2^{v}-i)={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\tau}-(2^{v}-i),

and since
i\leq 2^{v}
(assumption (ii)), we then have that
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\psi)}=i
by Lemma
2.10
. This proves the first statement, and we then have that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}=y2^{v}
, whence the second. ∎

All examples of anisotropic forms with nontrivial first higher isotropy index of which we are currently aware of are either of this type or stably of this type (i.e., acquire the desired shape over an extension that preserves the anisotropy of the form). We therefore formulate the following question, a positive integer to which would yield a refinement of Theorem
2.5
that takes the type invariant into account:

Question 10.8
.

Let
u
be the largest integer for which
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}
is divisible by
2^{u}
, and let
x
be the unique integer for which
x2^{u}<r\leq(x+1)2^{u}
. Is is then true that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r-x2^{u}
?

Theorem
2.5
gives the following:

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
u
be the largest integer for which
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}
is divisible by
2^{u}
, and let
x
be the unique integer for which
x2^{u}<r\leq(x+1)2^{u}
. Then
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=y2^{u}
for an odd integer
y\geq 2x+1
. In particular
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq 2(r-x2^{u})+s-2^{u}
.

Proof.

By hypothesis there is an odd integer
y
with
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=y2^{u}
. Set
\alpha:=r-x2^{u}
. Note that
\alpha>0
by definition. Now by Theorem
2.5
, we have
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq 2^{u}
, and so

y2^{u}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2r+s-\mathfrak{i}_{1}\geq 2r+s-2^{u}=(2x-1)2^{u}+2\alpha.

Since
y
is odd, the first statement follows. Then

\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2r+s-y2^{u}\leq 2r+s-(2x+1)2^{u}=2\alpha+s-2^{u},

and so the second statement also holds. ∎

Consider in particular the case where
s<2^{u}+\mathfrak{i}_{1}
. With the notation of the lemma, we then have that

(y-2x)2^{u}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}-(2x)2^{u}=2(r-x2^{u})+s-\mathfrak{i}_{1}<2(r-x2^{u})+2^{u}<3\cdot 2^{u}.

Since
y
is odd and at least
2x+1
, it follows that
y=2x+1
, i.e.,
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=(2x+1)2^{u}
. Put another way, we have

\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2(r-x2^{u})-(2^{u}-s).

Thus, when
s<2^{u}+\mathfrak{i}_{1}
, a positive answer to Question
10.8
amounts to a positive answer to the following:

Conjecture 10.10
.

Let
u
be the largest integer for which
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}
is divisible by
2^{u}
, and let
x
be the unique integer for which
x2^{u}<r\leq(x+1)2^{u}
. If
s<2^{u}+\mathfrak{i}_{1}
, then
r-x2^{u}\leq 2^{u}-s
.

In this direction, Theorem
10.4
allows us to say the following:

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
u
be the largest integer for which
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}
is divisible by
2^{u}
, and let
x
be th unique integer for which
x2^{u}<r\leq(x+1)2^{u}
. If
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}\geq s
, then
s<2^{u}
and
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r-x2^{u}\leq 2^{u}-s
. In particular, Conjecture
10.10
holds in this case.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Proposition
10.5
. Let
\psi
be a form of type
(r,1)
dominated by
\varphi
. Since
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\geq s
, we have that
\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{stb}}}{{\sim}}\psi
(Lemma
2.10
). In particular, we have that
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}
and
\Lambda^{U}(X)=\Lambda^{U}(X_{\psi})
(Lemma
2.10
, Corollary
8.6
). Let
j
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}
be the unique integers for which
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n_{1}}-2^{n_{2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{j-1}2^{n_{j}}
and
n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{j-1}>n_{j}+1
. By definition, we have
n_{j}=u
. Consider the integer

d_{j}:=\frac{{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}}{2}-2^{u-1}=\left(\frac{{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2\mathfrak{j}_{0}-\mathfrak{j}_{1}}{2}\right)+(-1)^{2j-1}2^{n_{j}-1}.

Since
\psi
is nondegenerate, and since
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\psi}={\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}
, Theorem
10.4
tells us that
(d_{j})_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda^{U}(X_{\psi})
. Since
\Lambda^{U}(X_{\psi})=\Lambda^{U}(X)
, Proposition
7.14
(2) then tells us that
\frac{{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}}{2}-2^{u-1}+\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r
. But since
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2r+s-\mathfrak{i}_{1}
, this says that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq 2^{u}-s
. In particular,
s<2^{u}
. By the remarks preceding the statement of Conjecture
10.10
, we then have that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2(r-x2^{u})-(2^{u}-s)
. Since
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq 2^{u}-s
, we then in fact have that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r-x2^{u}\leq 2^{u}-s
, as desired. ∎

The preceding result is enough, for instance, to completely describe the situation in which
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}\leq 9
:

Examples 10.12
.

Suppose
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}\leq 9
. We discuss the possible values of
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
and when they may occur. Note that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r
by Lemma
2.3
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}\in\{2,3,5,9\}
. In this case
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
by Corollary
2.6
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=4
. In this case,
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
is either
1
or
2
. If
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
, then
r=2
, so
\varphi
is nondegenerate of Knebusch height
1
. It is well-known that the nondegenerate anisotropic forms of dimension
4
and Knebusch height
1
are precisely the anisotropic general
2
-fold Pfister forms (
[
3
, Prop. 25.6]
), so
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
when
\varphi
is a general
2
-fold Pfister form, and
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
otherwise.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=6
. In this case,
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
is again either
1
or
2
by Corollary
2.6
. If
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour, then
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=2
by Lemma
2.11
(3). Conversely, if
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
, then
\varphi
has maximal splitting, and is hence a Pfister neighbour by
[
7
, Thm. 1.2]
. Thus,
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
if
\varphi
is a Pfister neighbour, and
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
otherwise. Note that in order to be in the first case, we require that
r\geq 2
, i.e., that
\varphi
is either nondegenerate or of type
(2,2)
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=8
. In this case,
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
is
1
,
2
or
4
by Theorem
2.5
. In order for
i_{1}
to be
4
, we must have that
r=4
, i.e., that
\varphi
is nondegenerate of Knebusch height
1
. Again, it is well-known that the nondegenerate anisotropic forms of dimension
8
and Knebusch height
1
are precisely the anisotropic
3
-fold general Pfister forms (
[
3
, Prop. 25.6]
). Next, if
\varphi\simeq{\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle}_{b}\otimes q
for some
a\in F^{\times}
and
4
-dimensional form
q
over
F
, then
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
is even (see the proof of Lemma
10.7
). Thus, if
\varphi
is not a general Pfister form (i.e., not divisible by a
2
-fold bilinear Pfister form), then
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
. Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
. Then
r\geq 2
, and so
\varphi
has type
(4,0)
,
(3,2)
or
(2,4)
. However, Theorem
10.11
shows that the second case is not possible, so
\varphi
is either nondegenerate or of type
(2,4)
. Consider first the case where
\varphi
is nondegenerate. If
\varphi\simeq\mathfrak{b}\otimes[1,a]
for some
4
-dimensional bilinear form
\mathfrak{b}
over
F
and
a\in F
, then
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
is even. Indeed, consider the separable quadratic extesion
K:=F[T]/(T^{2}+T+a)
. Since
[1,a]_{K}
is hyperbolic, so is
\varphi_{K}
, and hence
(\varphi_{1})_{K(\varphi)}
. By
[
3
, Cor. 23.6]
, it follows that
\varphi_{1}\simeq\mathfrak{c}\otimes[1,a]_{K}
for some symmetric bilinear form
\mathfrak{c}
over
K
. But
\varphi
has trivial discriminant, so the same is true of
\varphi_{1}
, and hence
\mathfrak{c}
must be of even-dimension, proving the claim. In particular, since
r=4
, we have
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}=2
provided that
\varphi
is not a general
3
-fold Pfister form. Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
. The
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=8-2
, and so
2_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda^{U}(X)
by Theorem
10.4
. By Proposition
7.16
, it follows that
\mathfrak{i}_{2}=2
. In view of the preceding discussion, this means that
\varphi_{1}
is a
2
-fold general Pfister form. In particular, the Clifford algebra of
\varphi_{1}
is Brauer equivalent to a quaternion algebra. By the index reduction theorem, the same is then true of the Clifford algebra of
\varphi
. In particular, there exists a
2
-fold general Pfister form
\tau
over
F
such that the
12
-dimensional form
\varphi\perp\tau
represents an element of
I^{3}_{q}(F)
. Scaling
\tau
if needed, we can assume that
\varphi\perp\tau
is isotropic. The anisotropic part of
\varphi\perp\tau
then has dimension at most
8
(
[
2
, Thm. 4.10]
), and so
\varphi\perp\tau\sim\pi
for a general
3
-fold Pfister form
\pi
over
F
(
[
3
, Cor. 25.12]
). Since
\pi\perp\tau\sim\varphi
(Lemma
2.1
),
\pi\perp\tau
is isotropic. By
[
3
, Thm. 24.2 and Prop. 24.1]
, it follows that there exist symmetric bilinear forms
\mathfrak{c}
and
\mathfrak{d}
over
F
, and an element
a\in F
such that
\pi\simeq\mathfrak{c}\otimes[1,a]
and
\tau\simeq\mathfrak{d}\otimes[1,a]
. If we again set
K:=F[T]/(T^{2}+T+a)
, we then get that
\varphi_{K}\sim\pi_{K}\perp\tau_{K}
is hyperbolic. By
[
3
, Cor. 23.6]
, it then follows that
\varphi\simeq\mathfrak{b}\otimes[1,a]
for a
4
-dimensional symmetric bilinear form
\mathfrak{b}
over
F
. Consider now the case where
\varphi
has type
(2,4)
. If
\varphi\simeq{\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle}_{b}\otimes q
for some
a\in F^{\times}
and form
q
of type
(1,2)
over
F
, then
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
is even by the argument in the proof of Lemma
10.7
. Since
r=2
, we must then have that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
. Conversely, if
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
, then
\varphi\simeq{\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle}_{b}\otimes q
for some
a\in F^{\times}
and form
q
of type
(1,2)
over
F
by
[
6
, Theorem 7.5]
. In summary:

•

\mathfrak{i}_{1}=4
if and only if
\varphi
is a general
3
-fold Pfister form;

•

\mathfrak{i}_{1}=2
if and only if
\varphi
is not a general
3
-fold Pfister form, and one of the following holds:

–

\varphi\simeq\mathfrak{b}\otimes[1,a]
for some
4
-dimensional symmetric bilinear form
\mathfrak{b}
of non-trivial determinant over
F
;

–

\varphi\simeq{\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle}_{b}\otimes q
for some
a\in F^{\times}
and some form
q
of type
(1,2)
over
F
.;

•

\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
in all other cases.

As far as Question
10.8
goes, the first open case appears in dimension
12
: If
\varphi
is a
12
-dimensional form of type
(3,6)
, we do not know if it is possible for
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
to equal
2
. Our expectation is that this is not possible, and we present in the next subsection a conjectural approach to this based on the methods of this article. This approach should allow to treat Conjecture
10.10
, as well as other cases of Question
10.8
in which
s
is small relative to
r
. In general, however, it is unclear to what extent the methods used here are applicable in situations where
s
is “large”. For example, we also do not know if
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
can equal
2
if
\varphi
is a
14
-dimensional form of type
(3,8)
or
(5,4)
. Unlike the situation for forms of type
(3,6)
, however, we do not have any clear approach to this problem at present.

10.E.
Excellent Connections for Degenerate Forms

We conclude this article by making the conjecture that Theorem
10.3
remains valid in the case where
\varphi
is degenerate:

Conjecture 10.13
.

Let
a
and
b
be integers with
0\leq a,d_{X}-b<r
. If the pair
(a,b)
is excellent, then
a_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
b^{\mathrm{up}}
are connected in
\Lambda(X)
.

The proof of Theorem
10.3
given in
[
17
]
is currently not transferrable to the degenerate setting, since although we have an action of cohomological-type Steenrod operations on
\overline{\mathrm{Ch}}(X\times X)
, we do not have an action such operations on
\mathrm{Ch}(X\times X)
itself. Over fields of characteristic different from
2
, however, there are Steenrod operations of
homological type
acting on the mod-2 Chow groups of any variety, smooth or not (see
[
1
]
or
[
3
, Ch. XI]
). We expect that analogous operations should exist in characteristic
2
, and that these operations are more or less sufficient to prove Conjecture
10.13
. We give here some implications of the validity of Conjecture
10.13
:

Proposition 10.14
.

Suppose that Conjecture
10.13
holds. Then:

(1)

Let
1\leq t<h
, and suppose that
\mathrm{MDT}(\varphi)
admits an element
\Lambda
with
a(\Lambda)=\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}
. Let
j
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}
be the unique nonnegative integers for which
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}-\mathfrak{j}_{t}=2^{n_{1}}-2^{n_{2}}+\cdots+(-1)^{j-1}2^{n_{j}}
and
n_{1}>n_{2}>\cdots>n_{j-1}>n_{j}+1
. For each integer
1\leq k\leq j
, consider the integer

d_{k}:=\left(\frac{{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}-2\mathfrak{j}_{t-1}-\mathfrak{j}_{t}}{2}\right)+\sum_{i=k}^{k}(-1)^{k+i-1}2^{n_{i}-1}.

If
d_{k}<r
, we then have that
(d_{k})_{\mathrm{lo}}\in\Lambda
. Moreover, if
t\leq t^{\prime}\leq h
is such that
\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}-1}<d_{k}\leq\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}
, then then
d_{k}+\mathfrak{i}_{t}\leq\mathfrak{j}_{t^{\prime}}
.

(2)

Write
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n}+m
for integers
n\geq 0
and
1\leq m\leq 2^{n}
. If
r<m
, then
{\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\varphi)}\leq m-r
.

(3)

Conjecture
10.10
holds.

Proof.

(1) Again, the second statement follows from the first and Proposition
7.14
(2). Given the validity of Conjecture
10.13
, however, the proof of Theorem
10.4
carries over verbatim.

Now, in proving the remaining statements, let first us note that since
a(\Lambda^{U}(X))=0=\mathfrak{j}_{0}
, we are in a position to apply (1) with
t=1
.

(2) Since
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r
, we have
\mathfrak{i}_{1}<m
, and hence
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}=2^{n+1}-d
for some integer
1\leq d<2^{n}
. Taking
t=1
in (1), we then have that the integer
d_{1}
is defined and equal to
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}-2^{n}=m-\mathfrak{i}_{1}
. If we had
m-\mathfrak{i}_{1}<r
, part (1) would then give that
m\leq r
, contradicting our assumption. We must therefore have that
m-\mathfrak{i}_{1}\geq r
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq m-r
.

(3) As in the statement of Conjecture
10.10
, let
u
be the largest integer for which
{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}
is divisible by
2^{u}
. Suppose that
s<2^{u}+\mathfrak{i}_{1}
. We again consider the statement of (1) with
t=1
. As in the proof of Theorem
10.11
, the integer
d_{j}
is equal to
\frac{{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}}{2}-2^{u-1}
. Since
s<2^{n}+\mathfrak{i}_{1}
, we have

\frac{{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}}{2}-2^{u-1}=\frac{2r+s-\mathfrak{i}_{1}}{2}-2^{u-1}=r+\frac{s-(2^{u}+\mathfrak{i}_{1})}{2}<r,

and so
\frac{{\mathrm{dim}_{\mathrm{Izh}}\!\;\varphi}}{2}-2^{u-1}+\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r
by (1). Exactly as in the proof of Theorem
10.11
, this then leads to the conclusion that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\leq r-x2^{u}\leq 2^{u}-s
.
∎

Remarks 10.15
.

(1)

The statement in part (1) of the preceding proposition may be interpreted as follows: Suppose
\psi
is an anisotropic nondegenerate form over an extension of
F
such that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\psi}={\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}
and
{\mathfrak{j}_{t}(\psi)}=\mathfrak{j}_{t}
for all
1\leq t\leq h
. The set
\Lambda(X)
may be viewed as a subset of
\Lambda(X_{\psi})
. We expect that any connections among elements of this subset that are forced to exist by the Knebusch splitting pattern of
\psi
(in particular, those given by the
t=1
case of Theorem
10.4
) remain valid in
\Lambda(X)
(now understood as an invariant of
\varphi
). More generally, recall that the shell pyramid diagram for
X
is the shell pyramid diagram for
X_{\psi}
, but with the shells indexed by integers
\geq h
deleted (see §
7.B
). Suppose that we have a restriction on the diagram for
X_{\psi}
that is entirely determined by the Knebusch splitting pattern of
\psi
. If we delete the shells indexed by integers
\geq h
, then we expect that what remains of the restriction is a valid restriction on the diagram for
X
.

(2)

Note the statement in part (2) implies that
\mathfrak{i}_{1}\neq 2
when
\varphi
is a
12
-dimensional form of type
(3,6)
(i.e., settles the first open case of Question
10.8
). This is not implied by Conjecture
10.10
, and so the scope of Conjecture
10.13
is broader. At the same time, the statement in part (1) says nothing about the problem of whether
\mathfrak{i}_{1}
can equal
2
when
\varphi
is a
14
-dimensional form of type
(3,8)
or
(5,4)
.

We conclude with the following lemma, which gives some meagre evidence for Conjecture
10.13
:

Lemma 10.16
.

Conjecture
10.13
holds in the case where
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}\leq 10
.

Proof.

In view of Theorem
10.3
, we may assume that
\varphi
is degenerate, i.e., that
s\geq 2
. We then have that
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}\geq 4
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=4
. In this case,
\varphi
has type
(1,2)
, and there are no excellent pairs to consider.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=5
. In this case,
\varphi
has type
(1,3)
, and the only excellent pair to consider is
(0,3)
. But in this case we have
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
(Examples
10.12
), and so
0_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
3^{\mathrm{up}}
are connected in
\Lambda(X)
by Proposition
7.13
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=6
. In this case,
\varphi
has type
(1,4)
or
(2,2)
. In the first case, however, there are no excellent pairs to consider, and so we can assume that
\varphi
has type
(2,2)
. Here, there are two excellent pairs to consider, namely
(0,3)
and
(1,4)
. But since
r=2
,
2
lies in the nondefective splitting pattern of
\varphi
. By Proposition
8.14
, it follows that
\varphi
is a virtual Pfister neighbour. But Lemma
8.13
then gives the desired connections in
\Lambda(X)
between
0_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
3^{\mathrm{up}}
as well as
1_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
4^{\mathrm{up}}
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=7
. In this case,
\varphi
has type
(1,5)
or
(2,3)
. In the first case, there are no excellent pairs to consider. Suppose therefore that
\varphi
has type
(2,3)
. Here, there is one excellent pair to consider, namely
(1,4)
. But since
\varphi
has type
(2,3)
, we have
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
(Examples
10.12
). Since
r=2
, we must then also have that
h=2
and
\mathfrak{i}_{2}=1
. By Proposition
7.13
, it then follows that
1_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
4^{\mathrm{up}}
are connected in
\Lambda(X)
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=8
. In this case,
\varphi
has type
(1,6)
,
(2,4)
or
(3,2)
. In the first two cases, there are no excellent pairs to consider. Suppose therefore that
\varphi
has type
(3,2)
. Then there are two excellent pairs to consider, namely
(1,4)
and
(2,5)
. Since
\varphi
has type
(3,2)
, we have
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
(Examples
10.12
). Then
\varphi_{1}
is a
6
-dimensional form of type
(1,3)
. By the preceding discussion,
0_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
3^{\mathrm{up}}
, as well as
1_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
4^{\mathrm{up}}
are then connected in
\Lambda(X_{\varphi_{1}})
. By Lemma
7.10
,
1_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
4^{\mathrm{up}}
, as well as
2_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
5^{\mathrm{up}}
are then connected in
\Lambda(X)
.

{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=9
. In this case,
\varphi
has type
(1,7)
,
(2,5)
or
(3,3)
. In all cases, we have the excellent pair
(0,7)
. But
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
in this case (Examples
10.12
), so
0_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
7^{\mathrm{up}}
are connected in
\Lambda(X)
by Proposition
7.13
. If
\varphi
has type
(1,7)
or
(2,5)
, then there are no other excellent pairs to consider. Suppose now that
\varphi
has type
(3,3)
. We then have one other excellent pair to consider, namely
(2,5)
. Again, however, since
\mathfrak{i}_{1}=1
,
\varphi_{1}
is a
7
-dimensional form of type
(2,3)
. By the preceding discussion,
1_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
4^{\mathrm{up}}
are then connected in
\Lambda(X_{\varphi_{1}})
. By Lemma
7.10
,
2_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
5^{\mathrm{up}}
are then connected in
\Lambda(X)
.

∎

When
{\mathrm{dim}\!\;\varphi}=10
, there is just one case in which we do not know the validity of Conjecture
10.13
, namely the case where
\varphi
has type
(3,4)
and nondefective splitting pattern
(1,3)
. Here,
2
is not in the nondefective splitting pattern of
\varphi
, and so
\varphi
is not a virtual Pfister neighbour. In particular, the argument used to handle the case of
6
-dimensional forms in the proof of the preceding lemma is not applicable here. Note that it is relatively straightforward to classify the anisotropic forms of type
(3,4)
and nondefective splitting pattern
(1,3)
: They are precisely the
10
-dimensional forms similar to
({\langle\!\langle a,b,c]]}\perp\langle 1,d,e,de\rangle)_{\mathrm{an}}
for some
a,b,c,d,e\in F^{\times}
. Thus, if we exclude this specific class of forms, then the statement of Conjecture
10.13
is also valid in dimension
10
.
